Although she was not physically injured, she was close enough to see the impact and stayed with the teens, trying to comfort them until emergency services arrived, later replaying the chaotic scene as she gave statements to police and local reporters. In the months that followed, she gradually withdrew from leading tours and avoided the intersection, but the impact of the incident seeped into other cherished routines at the senior center and church, straining her sense of purpose and connection. She finally agreed to see a mental health professional at the urging of her pastor and a longtime friend after they noticed she had stopped attending book club and bowling, and she admitted she could no longer enjoy the community activities that once defined her retirement.\n- Chief Complaint: I just don’t feel any interest in the community projects and hobbies that used to mean everything to me.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): Before the accident I lived for the walking tours, the garden, even silly little things like bowling night, but now when people invite me I just feel blank inside, like there’s no point in going because I know I won’t enjoy it the way I used to.\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): I used to be sharp enough to remember all the dates and stories for the tours, but these days I lose my train of thought in the middle of a conversation or stand in the kitchen staring at the pantry because I can’t decide something as simple as what to make for dinner.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere, especially when I hear tires screech or see a truck at a crosswalk, I’m right back on that corner watching that boy get hit, hearing the thud and the screaming so vividly that I feel sick and shaky even though I know I’m just standing on my own sidewalk.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I go the long way around town so I don’t have to pass that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if someone brings up car accidents or asks me about my old tour route, because even thinking about it makes my chest tighten.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep thinking that if I’d moved the group a little faster or insisted we wait for the next light, that boy might still be alive, and I can’t shake the feeling that I failed them, even when people tell me there was nothing more I could have done.\n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most days I walk around with this heavy mix of guilt and dread sitting in my stomach, like a dark cloud that doesn’t lift even when I’m at church or with my grandchildren, and it’s hard to remember what it felt like to be genuinely happy or at ease.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I make myself go to church or the senior center, I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass watching everyone else laugh and chat, and I just can’t seem to connect with them the way I used to, so I usually slip out early and go home alone.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Little things set me off now—if someone cuts me off in traffic or a friend is five minutes late, I snap at them or raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and afterward I feel ashamed and confused about why I got so worked up.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the nights when the memories won’t quiet down, I pour myself more wine than I should and sometimes drive faster than is sensible on the back roads, thinking, “Who cares what happens to me anyway,” even though I know that’s not really how I feel deep down.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I catch myself constantly checking the street before I step off the curb, watching cars like a hawk and tensing at every engine rev or loud noise, and even when I’m just sitting in a café I like to face the door so I can keep an eye on everything around me.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, I’ve stopped leading tours, dropped out of most of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving, so my days have shrunk down to mostly sitting at home alone and avoiding situations that used to give my life structure and meaning.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me ever since that truck hit those kids at the crosswalk a little over them the way I used to, so I usually slip out early and go home alone.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Little things set me off now—if someone cuts me off in traffic or a friend is five minutes late, I snap at them or raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and afterward I feel ashamed and confused about why I got so worked up.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the I wasn't physically hurt, but I stayed with them in the chaos trying to comfort them, and I had to keep replaying that awful scene for the police and reporters afterward.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any intense fear, helplessness, or horror during or immediately after the accident, or was your primary emotional response guilt and self-blame from the beginning?\n\nPatient: During the accident itself, it was absolute horror, panic, and chaos as I tried to comfort those injured kids in the street. But almost immediately afterward, the intense guilt and self-blame took over, and thinking I should have walked faster or waited for the next light has become the heaviest, most persistent feeling I carry.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time since the accident where your feelings of guilt and self-blame have decreased or seemed more manageable, or have they remained relatively constant over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't gotten any better; they have remained constant and heavy over the last two years. It feels like a persistent dark cloud that just won't lift, and I constantly struggle with the feeling that I failed those kids, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any specific triggers, such as anniversaries of the accident, news reports, or encounters with reminders of the event, that make your feelings of guilt, blankness, or horror worsen, or if these feelings are consistently present regardless of the situation?\n\nPatient: While that heavy guilt is always there in the background, certain triggers make things much worse. Hearing screeching tires or seeing a truck at a crosswalk puts me right back on that corner, making me feel sick and shaky. Even hearing someone talk about car accidents or my old tour route makes my chest tighten, so I go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection."

I wasn't physically hurt, but I stayed with them in the chaos trying to comfort them, and I had to keep replaying that awful scene for the police and reporters afterward.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any intense fear, helplessness, or horror during or immediately after the accident, or was your primary emotional response guilt and self-blame from the beginning?\n\nPatient: During the accident itself, it was absolute horror, panic, and chaos as I tried to comfort those injured kids in the street. But almost immediately afterward, the intense guilt and self-blame took over, and thinking I should have walked faster or waited for the next light has become the heaviest, most persistent feeling I carry.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time since the accident where your feelings of guilt and self-blame have decreased or seemed more manageable, or have they remained relatively constant over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't gotten any better; they have remained constant and heavy over the last two years. It feels like a persistent dark cloud that just won't lift, and I constantly struggle with the feeling that I failed those kids, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any specific triggers, such as anniversaries of the accident, news reports, or encounters with reminders of the event, that make your feelings of guilt, blankness, or horror worsen, or if these feelings are consistently present regardless of the situation?\n\nPatient: While that heavy guilt is always there in the background, certain triggers make things much worse. Hearing screeching tires or seeing a truck at a crosswalk puts me right back on that corner, making me feel sick and shaky. Even hearing someone talk about car accidents or my old tour route makes my chest tighten, so I go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you avoid that intersection and other reminders of the accident, and if these avoidance behaviors help reduce your feelings of guilt and anxiety, or do they seem to have little effect on your overall emotional state?\n\nPatient: I take the long way around town to completely avoid that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if anyone mentions car accidents because my chest starts to tighten. While avoiding these things stops me from panicking in the moment, it doesn't help my overall state; I still carry that heavy, constant mix of guilt and dread every single day."
  }
]

Although she was not physically injured, she was close enough to see the impact and stayed with the teens, trying to comfort them until emergency services arrived, later replaying the chaotic scene as she gave statements to police and local reporters. In the months that followed, she gradually withdrew from leading tours and avoided the intersection, but the impact of the incident seeped into other cherished routines at the senior center and church, straining her sense of purpose and connection. She finally agreed to see a mental health professional at the urging of her pastor and a longtime friend after they noticed she had stopped attending book club and bowling, and she admitted she could no longer enjoy the community activities that once defined her retirement.\n- Chief Complaint: I just don’t feel any interest in the community projects and hobbies that used to mean everything to me.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): Before the accident I lived for the walking tours, the garden, even silly little things like bowling night, but now when people invite me I just feel blank inside, like there’s no point in going because I know I won’t enjoy it the way I used to.\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): I used to be sharp enough to remember all the dates and stories for the tours, but these days I lose my train of thought in the middle of a conversation or stand in the kitchen staring at the pantry because I can’t decide something as simple as what to make for dinner.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere, especially when I hear tires screech or see a truck at a crosswalk, I’m right back on that corner watching that boy get hit, hearing the thud and the screaming so vividly that I feel sick and shaky even though I know I’m just standing on my own sidewalk.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I go the long way around town so I don’t have to pass that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if someone brings up car accidents or asks me about my old tour route, because even thinking about it makes my chest tighten.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep thinking that if I’d moved the group a little faster or insisted we wait for the next light, that boy might still be alive, and I can’t shake the feeling that I failed them, even when people tell me there was nothing more I could have done.\n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most days I walk around with this heavy mix of guilt and dread sitting in my stomach, like a dark cloud that doesn’t lift even when I’m at church or with my grandchildren, and it’s hard to remember what it felt like to be genuinely happy or at ease.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I make myself go to church or the senior center, I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass watching everyone else laugh and chat, and I just can’t seem to connect with them the way I used to, so I usually slip out early and go home alone.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Little things set me off now—if someone cuts me off in traffic or a friend is five minutes late, I snap at them or raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and afterward I feel ashamed and confused about why I got so worked up.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the nights when the memories won’t quiet down, I pour myself more wine than I should and sometimes drive faster than is sensible on the back roads, thinking, “Who cares what happens to me anyway,” even though I know that’s not really how I feel deep down.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I catch myself constantly checking the street before I step off the curb, watching cars like a hawk and tensing at every engine rev or loud noise, and even when I’m just sitting in a café I like to face the door so I can keep an eye on everything around me.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, I’ve stopped leading tours, dropped out of most of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving, so my days have shrunk down to mostly sitting at home alone and avoiding situations that used to give my life structure and meaning.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me ever since that truck hit those kids at the crosswalk a little over     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: I just don’t feel any interest in the community projects and hobbies that used to mean everything to me. My pastor and a close friend noticed I've stopped participating in my usual groups and urged me to seek help. I just feel completely blank inside when I try to do the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or guilt along with this loss of interest in activities, or is it more of a flat, empty feeling without any other strong emotions?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both, because while I feel completely blank and empty when I think about my old hobbies, underneath there is this constant, heavy guilt. I keep replaying that awful day and blaming myself, thinking that if I had just moved the group faster or waited for the next light, those teenagers would be safe and I wouldn't have failed them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about that awful day you're referring to, what happened and how long ago was it?\n\nPatient: It was about two years and three months ago when I was leading an evening historic walking tour in downtown Belvidere, and a speeding pickup truck ran a red light at a crosswalk. It struck a group of teenagers, killing one boy and badly injuring another girl I knew from our community garden. I wasn't physically hurt, but I stayed with them in the chaos trying to comfort them, and I had to keep replaying that awful scene for the police and reporters afterward.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any intense fear, helplessness, or horror during or immediately after the accident, or was your primary emotional response guilt and self-blame from the beginning?\n\nPatient: During the accident itself, it was absolute horror, panic, and chaos as I tried to comfort those injured kids in the street. But almost immediately afterward, the intense guilt and self-blame took over, and thinking I should have walked faster or waited for the next light has become the heaviest, most persistent feeling I carry.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time since the accident where your feelings of guilt and self-blame have decreased or seemed more manageable, or have they remained relatively constant over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't gotten any better; they have remained constant and heavy over the last two years. It feels like a persistent dark cloud that just won't lift, and I constantly struggle with the feeling that I failed those kids, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any specific triggers, such as anniversaries of the accident, news reports, or encounters with reminders of the event, that make your feelings of guilt, blankness, or horror worsen, or if these feelings are consistently present regardless of the situation?\n\nPatient: While that heavy guilt is always there in the background, certain triggers make things much worse. Hearing screeching tires or seeing a truck at a crosswalk puts me right back on that corner, making me feel sick and shaky. Even hearing someone talk about car accidents or my old tour route makes my chest tighten, so I go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you avoid that intersection and other reminders of the accident, and if these avoidance behaviors help reduce your feelings of guilt and anxiety, or do they seem to have little effect on your overall emotional state?\n\nPatient: I take the long way around town to completely avoid that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if anyone mentions car accidents because my chest starts to tighten. While avoiding these things stops me from panicking in the moment, it doesn't help my overall state; I still carry that heavy, constant mix of guilt and dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience vivid, intrusive memories or flashbacks of the accident, where you feel like you're reliving the event, in addition to the guilt and emotional numbness you've described?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely do. Out of nowhere, especially if I hear tires screech or see a truck at a crosswalk, I'm suddenly right back on that corner hearing the thud and the screaming all over again. It feels so incredibly vivid that it leaves me feeling completely sick and shaky, even though I know I'm actually safe on the sidewalk.\n\nDoctor: During these flashbacks, do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or detached from the experience, as if it's happening to someone else?"

hearing the thud and the screaming so vividly that I feel sick and shaky even though I know I’m just standing on my own sidewalk.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I go the long way around town so I don’t have to pass that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if someone brings up car accidents or asks me about my old tour route, because even thinking about it makes my chest tighten.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep thinking that if I’d moved the group a little faster or insisted we wait for the next light, that boy might still be alive, and I can’t shake the feeling that I failed them, even when people tell me there was nothing more I could have done.\n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Most days I walk around with this heavy mix of guilt and dread sitting in my stomach, like a dark cloud that doesn’t lift even when I’m at church or with my grandchildren, and it’s hard to remember what it felt like to be genuinely happy or at ease.\n- S075 (Social_Detachment): Even when I make myself go to church or the senior center, I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass watching everyone else laugh and chat, and I just can’t seem to connect with them the way I used to, so I usually slip out early and go home alone.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Little things set me off now—if someone cuts me off in traffic or a friend is five minutes late, I snap at them or raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and afterward I feel ashamed and confused about why I got so worked up.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): On the nights when the memories won’t quiet down, I pour myself more wine than I should and sometimes drive faster than is sensible on the back roads, thinking, “Who cares what happens to me anyway,” even though I know that’s not really how I feel deep down.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I catch myself constantly checking the street before I step off the curb, watching cars like a hawk and tensing at every engine rev or loud noise, and even when I’m just sitting in a café I like to face the door so I can keep an eye on everything around me.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, I’ve stopped leading tours, dropped out of most of my church and senior center activities, and even cut back on driving, so my days have shrunk down to mostly sitting at home alone and avoiding situations that used to give my life structure and meaning.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me ever since that truck hit those kids at the crosswalk a little over Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: I just don’t feel any interest in the community projects and hobbies that used to mean everything to me. My pastor and a close friend noticed I've stopped participating in my usual groups and urged me to seek help. I just feel completely blank inside when I try to do the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or guilt along with this loss of interest in activities, or is it more of a flat, empty feeling without any other strong emotions?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both, because while I feel completely blank and empty when I think about my old hobbies, underneath there is this constant, heavy guilt. I keep replaying that awful day and blaming myself, thinking that if I had just moved the group faster or waited for the next light, those teenagers would be safe and I wouldn't have failed them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about that awful day you're referring to, what happened and how long ago was it?\n\nPatient: It was about two years and three months ago when I was leading an evening historic walking tour in downtown Belvidere, and a speeding pickup truck ran a red light at a crosswalk. It struck a group of teenagers, killing one boy and badly injuring another girl I knew from our community garden. I wasn't physically hurt, but I stayed with them in the chaos trying to comfort them, and I had to keep replaying that awful scene for the police and reporters afterward.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any intense fear, helplessness, or horror during or immediately after the accident, or was your primary emotional response guilt and self-blame from the beginning?\n\nPatient: During the accident itself, it was absolute horror, panic, and chaos as I tried to comfort those injured kids in the street. But almost immediately afterward, the intense guilt and self-blame took over, and thinking I should have walked faster or waited for the next light has become the heaviest, most persistent feeling I carry.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time since the accident where your feelings of guilt and self-blame have decreased or seemed more manageable, or have they remained relatively constant over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't gotten any better; they have remained constant and heavy over the last two years. It feels like a persistent dark cloud that just won't lift, and I constantly struggle with the feeling that I failed those kids, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any specific triggers, such as anniversaries of the accident, news reports, or encounters with reminders of the event, that make your feelings of guilt, blankness, or horror worsen, or if these feelings are consistently present regardless of the situation?\n\nPatient: While that heavy guilt is always there in the background, certain triggers make things much worse. Hearing screeching tires or seeing a truck at a crosswalk puts me right back on that corner, making me feel sick and shaky. Even hearing someone talk about car accidents or my old tour route makes my chest tighten, so I go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you avoid that intersection and other reminders of the accident, and if these avoidance behaviors help reduce your feelings of guilt and anxiety, or do they seem to have little effect on your overall emotional state?\n\nPatient: I take the long way around town to completely avoid that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if anyone mentions car accidents because my chest starts to tighten. While avoiding these things stops me from panicking in the moment, it doesn't help my overall state; I still carry that heavy, constant mix of guilt and dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience vivid, intrusive memories or flashbacks of the accident, where you feel like you're reliving the event, in addition to the guilt and emotional numbness you've described?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely do. Out of nowhere, especially if I hear tires screech or see a truck at a crosswalk, I'm suddenly right back on that corner hearing the thud and the screaming all over again. It feels so incredibly vivid that it leaves me feeling completely sick and shaky, even though I know I'm actually safe on the sidewalk.\n\nDoctor: During these flashbacks, do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or detached from the experience, as if it's happening to someone else?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like I'm outside my body or detached at all. In fact, it's the exact opposite—I feel very much in my body and the experience is terrifyingly real, like I am right back on that corner in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a daze or detached from the world around you after the accident, as if things weren't real, or has that not been part of your experience?"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: I just don’t feel any interest in the community projects and hobbies that used to mean everything to me. My pastor and a close friend noticed I've stopped participating in my usual groups and urged me to seek help. I just feel completely blank inside when I try to do the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or guilt along with this loss of interest in activities, or is it more of a flat, empty feeling without any other strong emotions?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both, because while I feel completely blank and empty when I think about my old hobbies, underneath there is this constant, heavy guilt. I keep replaying that awful day and blaming myself, thinking that if I had just moved the group faster or waited for the next light, those teenagers would be safe and I wouldn't have failed them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about that awful day you're referring to, what happened and how long ago was it?\n\nPatient: It was about two years and three months ago when I was leading an evening historic walking tour in downtown Belvidere, and a speeding pickup truck ran a red light at a crosswalk. It struck a group of teenagers, killing one boy and badly injuring another girl I knew from our community garden. I wasn't physically hurt, but I stayed with them in the chaos trying to comfort them, and I had to keep replaying that awful scene for the police and reporters afterward.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any intense fear, helplessness, or horror during or immediately after the accident, or was your primary emotional response guilt and self-blame from the beginning?\n\nPatient: During the accident itself, it was absolute horror, panic, and chaos as I tried to comfort those injured kids in the street. But almost immediately afterward, the intense guilt and self-blame took over, and thinking I should have walked faster or waited for the next light has become the heaviest, most persistent feeling I carry.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time since the accident where your feelings of guilt and self-blame have decreased or seemed more manageable, or have they remained relatively constant over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't gotten any better; they have remained constant and heavy over the last two years. It feels like a persistent dark cloud that just won't lift, and I constantly struggle with the feeling that I failed those kids, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any specific triggers, such as anniversaries of the accident, news reports, or encounters with reminders of the event, that make your feelings of guilt, blankness, or horror worsen, or if these feelings are consistently present regardless of the situation?\n\nPatient: While that heavy guilt is always there in the background, certain triggers make things much worse. Hearing screeching tires or seeing a truck at a crosswalk puts me right back on that corner, making me feel sick and shaky. Even hearing someone talk about car accidents or my old tour route makes my chest tighten, so I go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you avoid that intersection and other reminders of the accident, and if these avoidance behaviors help reduce your feelings of guilt and anxiety, or do they seem to have little effect on your overall emotional state?\n\nPatient: I take the long way around town to completely avoid that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if anyone mentions car accidents because my chest starts to tighten. While avoiding these things stops me from panicking in the moment, it doesn't help my overall state; I still carry that heavy, constant mix of guilt and dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience vivid, intrusive memories or flashbacks of the accident, where you feel like you're reliving the event, in addition to the guilt and emotional numbness you've described?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely do. Out of nowhere, especially if I hear tires screech or see a truck at a crosswalk, I'm suddenly right back on that corner hearing the thud and the screaming all over again. It feels so incredibly vivid that it leaves me feeling completely sick and shaky, even though I know I'm actually safe on the sidewalk.\n\nDoctor: During these flashbacks, do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or detached from the experience, as if it's happening to someone else?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like I'm outside my body or detached at all. In fact, it's the exact opposite—I feel very much in my body and the experience is terrifyingly real, like I am right back on that corner in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a daze or detached from the world around you after the accident, as if things weren't real, or has that not been part of your experience?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced feeling like things around me aren't real or being in that kind of a daze. However, I do feel very disconnected from other people, almost like I'm behind a pane of glass watching everyone else live their lives.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling disconnected from others like you're behind a pane of glass, but can you tell me if you've also felt disconnected from yourself or your emotions at any point since the accident, or is this feeling more about being disconnected from other people and the world around you?"

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: I just don’t feel any interest in the community projects and hobbies that used to mean everything to me. My pastor and a close friend noticed I've stopped participating in my usual groups and urged me to seek help. I just feel completely blank inside when I try to do the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or guilt along with this loss of interest in activities, or is it more of a flat, empty feeling without any other strong emotions?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both, because while I feel completely blank and empty when I think about my old hobbies, underneath there is this constant, heavy guilt. I keep replaying that awful day and blaming myself, thinking that if I had just moved the group faster or waited for the next light, those teenagers would be safe and I wouldn't have failed them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about that awful day you're referring to, what happened and how long ago was it?\n\nPatient: It was about two years and three months ago when I was leading an evening historic walking tour in downtown Belvidere, and a speeding pickup truck ran a red light at a crosswalk. It struck a group of teenagers, killing one boy and badly injuring another girl I knew from our community garden. I wasn't physically hurt, but I stayed with them in the chaos trying to comfort them, and I had to keep replaying that awful scene for the police and reporters afterward.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any intense fear, helplessness, or horror during or immediately after the accident, or was your primary emotional response guilt and self-blame from the beginning?\n\nPatient: During the accident itself, it was absolute horror, panic, and chaos as I tried to comfort those injured kids in the street. But almost immediately afterward, the intense guilt and self-blame took over, and thinking I should have walked faster or waited for the next light has become the heaviest, most persistent feeling I carry.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of time since the accident where your feelings of guilt and self-blame have decreased or seemed more manageable, or have they remained relatively constant over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't gotten any better; they have remained constant and heavy over the last two years. It feels like a persistent dark cloud that just won't lift, and I constantly struggle with the feeling that I failed those kids, no matter what anyone else tells me.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any specific triggers, such as anniversaries of the accident, news reports, or encounters with reminders of the event, that make your feelings of guilt, blankness, or horror worsen, or if these feelings are consistently present regardless of the situation?\n\nPatient: While that heavy guilt is always there in the background, certain triggers make things much worse. Hearing screeching tires or seeing a truck at a crosswalk puts me right back on that corner, making me feel sick and shaky. Even hearing someone talk about car accidents or my old tour route makes my chest tighten, so I go completely out of my way to avoid that intersection.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you avoid that intersection and other reminders of the accident, and if these avoidance behaviors help reduce your feelings of guilt and anxiety, or do they seem to have little effect on your overall emotional state?\n\nPatient: I take the long way around town to completely avoid that intersection, and I immediately shut down or change the subject if anyone mentions car accidents because my chest starts to tighten. While avoiding these things stops me from panicking in the moment, it doesn't help my overall state; I still carry that heavy, constant mix of guilt and dread every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience vivid, intrusive memories or flashbacks of the accident, where you feel like you're reliving the event, in addition to the guilt and emotional numbness you've described?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely do. Out of nowhere, especially if I hear tires screech or see a truck at a crosswalk, I'm suddenly right back on that corner hearing the thud and the screaming all over again. It feels so incredibly vivid that it leaves me feeling completely sick and shaky, even though I know I'm actually safe on the sidewalk.\n\nDoctor: During these flashbacks, do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or detached from the experience, as if it's happening to someone else?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like I'm outside my body or detached at all. In fact, it's the exact opposite—I feel very much in my body and the experience is terrifyingly real, like I am right back on that corner in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a daze or detached from the world around you after the accident, as if things weren't real, or has that not been part of your experience?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced feeling like things around me aren't real or being in that kind of a daze. However, I do feel very disconnected from other people, almost like I'm behind a pane of glass watching everyone else live their lives.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling disconnected from others like you're behind a pane of glass, but can you tell me if you've also felt disconnected from yourself or your emotions at any point since the accident, or is this feeling more about being disconnected from other people and the world around you?\n\nPatient: The feeling of being behind a pane of glass is really about feeling disconnected from other people, like I'm watching their warmth and conversations but just can't join in. I don't feel detached from my physical self, but I do feel emotionally empty or \"blank\" inside. That emptiness makes it very hard to feel genuine happiness or relate to anyone the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments or days where you feel a slight return of your previous emotional responsiveness or interest in activities, even if it's fleeting, or has your emotional state remained consistently flat and unresponsive since the accident?"
  }

No, my emotional state has been consistently flat and unresponsive ever since the accident. I just feel completely blank inside, and this heavy cloud of guilt and dread makes it impossible to remember what it feels like to be genuinely happy or interested in anything, even for a fleeting moment.

In fact, it's the exact opposite—I feel very much in my body and the experience is terrifyingly real, like I am right back on that corner in that exact moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a daze or detached from the world around you after the accident, as if things weren't real, or has that not been part of your experience?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced feeling like things around me aren't real or being in that kind of a daze. However, I do feel very disconnected from other people, almost like I'm behind a pane of glass watching everyone else live their lives.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling disconnected from others like you're behind a pane of glass, but can you tell me if you've also felt disconnected from yourself or your emotions at any point since the accident, or is this feeling more about being disconnected from other people and the world around you?\n\nPatient: The feeling of being behind a pane of glass is really about feeling disconnected from other people, like I'm watching their warmth and conversations but just can't join in. I don't feel detached from my physical self, but I do feel emotionally empty or \"blank\" inside. That emptiness makes it very hard to feel genuine happiness or relate to anyone the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments or days where you feel a slight return of your previous emotional responsiveness or interest in activities, even if it's fleeting, or has your emotional state remained consistently flat and unresponsive since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, my emotional state has been consistently flat and unresponsive ever since the accident. I just feel completely blank inside, and this heavy cloud of guilt and dread makes it impossible to remember what it feels like to be genuinely happy or interested in anything, even for a fleeting moment."
  }

Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
